32.5\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3540 cell en0052\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3541 text 79.0\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3542 text Detailed analysis of prediction accuracy across different regions at varying time steps revealed a distinct trend: as the prediction step length increased, prediction error for high-elevation nodes decreased, while error for medium-elevation nodes increased. This indicates that for high-elevation nodes experiencing data scarcity, extending the time series length and incorporating more prior temporal information enhances forecast accuracy. Conversely, for nodes in data-rich areas, additional temporal information may introduce unnecessary noise or redundancy, reducing model accuracy. This finding suggests that, for nodes in data-scarce conditions, incorporating more prior information and adopting more suitable data fusion techniques will be key to enhancing the model’s performance.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3543 container sec0015\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3544 heading 4.2. Results explanation, Value: 3, ID: cesectitle0020\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3545 text 4.2 .  Results explanation\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3546 container sec0016\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3547 heading 4.2.1. Rule extraction from recurrent layer, Value: 4, ID: cesectitle0021\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3548 text 4.2.1 .  Rule extraction from recurrent layer\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3549 container para0048\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3550 text Employing recurrent graph convolution (as shown in \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3551 link Description: Eq. (5), Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3552 text ), the AGN model gradually expands the receptive field of sensor nodes’ neighboring nodes. Simultaneously, pressure feature transfer from sensor nodes to global nodes is achieved by the self-attention mechanism, enabling long-range generalization.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3553 container para0049\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3554 text To understand the rule of recurrent layers and their impact on the model’s generalization ability, this study varied the number of recurrent layers and tested the model’s generalization accuracy under different iterations. \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3555 link Description: Fig. 5, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3556 text (a) shows the relative position relationship between sensor nodes and non-sensor nodes. The size and color depth of the non-sensor nodes indicate their distance from the nearest sensor nodes. Additionally, the data statistics in the upper right corner reveal the shortest path distribution between sensor nodes and non-sensor nodes. Notably, most non-sensor nodes are three steps away from sensor nodes, with the longest path being eleven steps. This information provides a crucial basis for subsequent analysis.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3557 container fig0005\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3558 image Fig 5\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3559 content list\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3560 link Description: Download: Download high-res image (827KB), Help: Download high-res image (827KB), Value: ars.els-cdn.com/con…\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3561 link Description: Download: Download full-size image, Help: Download full-size image, Value: ars.els-cdn.com/con…\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3562 container spara005\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3563 text Fig. 5 .  Analysis of the number of the recurrent layers: (a) shortest path from sensor nodes to non-sensor nodes; (b) MAE distribution corresponding to different numbers of the recurrent layers; (c) MAPE corresponding to different numbers of the recurrent layers.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3564 container para0050\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3565 text Experimental results (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3566 link Description: Figs. 5, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3567 text (b) and 5(c)) indicate that the best performance is not achieved at the maximum iteration of 11, but at 3 iterations of recurrent layers. This finding demonstrates that more iterations are not necessarily better; instead, they need to match the majority of the shortest path between sensor nodes and non-sensor nodes. When the number of iterations exceeds a certain threshold (such as 1 in this case), further increasing the number of recurrent layers has limited improvement on the model’s prediction performance. This is primarily due to the attention mechanism’s ability to capture long-distance correlations, allowing nodes to transcend physical distance limitations. Through the self-attention mechanism, the model can learn and transfer the pressure characteristics from sensor nodes to all nodes, rather than being limited to the sensor nodes’ neighborhood. Therefore, spatial generalizing process can be summarized as two significant mechanisms: neighbor topology feature transferring based on graph convolution, and global feature generalization based on the self-attention mechanism. Based on these two key mechanisms, the AGN model can generalize the learned graph structure-related features to all nodes in WDS, achieving state forecasting.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3568 text In the GGNN, ensuring sensor nodes can “perceive” the farthest non-sensor nodes theoretically requires up to 11 steps of recurrent iterations. However, this approach presents significant practical challenges, particularly due to the substantial increase in training time. For example, training with 11 iterations of recurrent layers on an A10 tensor core GPU in the small-scale network studied here takes about two days. This duration is unacceptable for practical applications in large-scale WDSs, where non-sensor nodes are further away from their nearest sensor node. In contrast, using 3 recurrent layers reduces the training time to about 3 h, demonstrating significant efficiency advantages.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3569 text Further analysis reveals that increasing the number of recurrent layers from 1 to 2 significantly reduces the MAPE. However, increasing beyond 3 layers leads to a rise in MAPE. This suggests that too many recurrent layers may introduce excessive non-sensor node features, causing data redundancy or pollution, and weakening the model’s prediction performance. Additionally, regardless of the number of recurrent layers, the cumulative maximum MAE remains stable at 96.03 %, revealing an optimization bottleneck in specific regions, such as low-elevation region in this study. These regions, due to insufficient sensor placement, cannot improve training results by increasing the number of recurrent layers.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3570 text In summary, incorporating the self-attention mechanism in the recurrent layers significantly enhances the model’s prediction accuracy. It also effectively reduces the iteration of recurrent layers, greatly decreasing training time of the model. This advantage is crucial for predictive modeling of actual WDSs, especially for medium- and large-scale systems, where its efficiency will provide significant application value.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3571 container sec0017\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3572 heading 4.2.2. Hidden semantic analysis of gating mechanisms, Value: 4, ID: cesectitle0022\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3573 text 4.2.2 .  Hidden semantic analysis of gating mechanisms\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3574 container para0054\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3575 text Through an in-depth analysis of the weight matrices obtained from the trained reset gate (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3576 link Description: Eq. (6), Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3577 text ) and the update gate (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3578 link Description: Eq. (7), Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3579 text ), this study reveals the unique mechanism by which the model selectively retains short- and long-term information between nodes. Analysis of the model parameters reveals that the weights of the two critical gates consistently remain close to 0.5. The balance between the reset gate and update gate indicates that short- and long-term memory are equally important in the forecasting model. This finding inspires a new perspective on model simplification: replacing complex gating mechanisms with several constants. This operation will significantly reduce the model’s parameters, thereby enhancing its performance and facilitating its application in larger-scale WDSs.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3580 container para0055\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3581 text The CHPS (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3582 link Description: Eq. (8), Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3583 text ) demonstrates how the AGN model efficiently retains key information. By visually mapping the attention weight of the hidden state layer onto the network (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3584 link Description: Fig. 6, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3585 text ), we can observe how sensor nodes influence non-sensor nodes and how the model selectively focuses on feature correlation. For visualization purposes, only edges with weights greater than 0.01 are displayed in the figure, and the weights of nodes 215 were divided by 10. This analysis deepens our understanding of the model’s internal hidden mechanisms and highlights the impact of sensor layout on prediction performance.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3586 container fig0006\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3587 image Fig 6\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3588 content list\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3589 link Description: Download: Download high-res image (2MB), Help: Download high-res image (2MB), Value: ars.els-cdn.com/con…\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3590 link Description: Download: Download full-size image, Help: Download full-size image, Value: ars.els-cdn.com/con…\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3591 container spara006\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3592 text Fig. 6 .  Attention weight of the CHPS.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3593 text The study found significant differences in the contributions of various sensor nodes during the prediction process, reflecting their interdependence and the strategic significance of sensor layout. Intuitively, sensor nodes in central areas (e.g., 458, 549, and 752) enjoy higher weights due to their critical positions, significantly enhancing the overall prediction performance. In contrast, sensors at the network periphery (e.g., nodes 114, 469, and 769) are less important. The concepts of geographical center and periphery do not exist in graph theory; only the concept of connectivity does. In other words, nodes that exhibit better connectivity or play a crucial role in the network’s connectivity were trained with higher weights. Placing sensor nodes at critical connectivity points appears to be key to improving the performance of graph-based data-driven models. The importance related to node connectivity underscores the need to optimize sensor layouts, especially in selecting critical connections. However, further cross-network studies are needed to definitively establish the correlation between sensor placement and connectivity.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3594 text By introducing the self-attention mechanism into the gating mechanism, this study broadens the receptive field of sensor nodes and effectively captures global information. Additionally, it deepens our understanding of the working mechanisms of complex network prediction models. These findings provide theoretical support for optimizing existing models and lay a solid foundation for developing new models for future urban infrastructure monitoring and management.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3595 container sec0018\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3596 heading 4.2.3. Attribution analysis based on nodal features, Value: 4, ID: cesectitle0023\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3597 text 4.2.3 .  Attribution analysis based on nodal features\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3598 container para0058\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3599 text By separately masking nodal height and nodal type features, this study analyzed their independent and synergistic effects on the model’s prediction. \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3600 link Description: Fig. 7, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3601 text  shows the prediction effects after masking these two features in the AGN model. The fourth quadrant of the figure summarizes the MAE and MAPE statistics for each masking scenario, providing quantitative evidence of feature contributions. The AGN model in \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3602 link Description: Fig. 7, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3603 text (a), with both nodal type and height masked, is a modification of the GGNN (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3604 link Description: Fig. 3, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3605 text (c)), replacing all fully connected layers with self-attention layers. Experimental results show that the attention mechanism significantly expands the model’s receptive field, enabling 50.1 % of the nodes to be trained — a 14.7 % improvement over the original GGNN. These findings highlight the attention mechanism’s advantage in capturing long-distance correlations between nodes.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3606 container fig0007\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3607 image Fig 7\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3608 content list\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3609 link Description: Download: Download high-res image (1009KB), Help: Download high-res image (1009KB), Value: ars.els-cdn.com/con…\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3610 link Description: Download: Download full-size image, Help: Download full-size image, Value: ars.els-cdn.com/con…\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3611 container spara007\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3612 text Fig. 7 .  Nodal features analysis: (a) Mask nodal type and nodal elevation features; (b) Mask nodal type features; (c) Mask nodal elevation features.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3613 container para0059\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3614 text Adding any individual nodal feature to the “AGN(-type)(-height)” scenario can significantly enhance the model’s overall prediction accuracy. Specifically, adding only the nodal height feature (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3615 link Description: Fig. 7, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3616 text (b)) increases the proportion of nodes with MAE<1.0 m from 0 % to 95.6 %. This indicates that height, a key indicator of spatial location information, provides extensive and fundamental predictive support for the model. Adding nodal type features (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3617 link Description: Fig. 7, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3618 text (c)) increases the proportion of nodes with MAE<0.5 m from 0 % to 23.2 %. This significantly enhances the model’s ability to capture specific nodal characteristics and improves prediction accuracy, demonstrating the unique value of type features in refining predictions. Both nodal elevation and type features offer distinct and complementary contributions to model performance. The results highlight the critical role of prior network information in improving model performance.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3619 text However, in water networks lacking detailed nodal information, obtaining accurate nodal height and type data can be challenging. Therefore, promoting and deepening the AGN model in practical applications will require diversified model assistance and data substitution strategies. For instance, nodal height can be approximated using surface elevation, and nodal type can be inferred by integrating urban AOI data with network topology. With the inclusion and integration of more network prior information, the AGN model can better align with water network characteristics, becoming powerful tools for the safety management and distribution optimization of WDSs.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t3620 container sec0019\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3621 heading 5. Conclusions, Value: 2, ID: cesectitle0024\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3622 text 5 .  Conclusions\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3623 text This study proposes a novel multi-step demand forecasting solution for cities lacking hydraulic model support. By integrating forecasting and interpretability into a single, cohesive framework, it provides accurate and transparent predictions. Compared to other forecasting methods not reliant on hydraulic models, this study demonstrates the superiority of the proposed AGN method in both spatial generalization performance and its long-term accuracy in temporal forecasting performance. The high accuracy achieved further validates the feasibility of predicting water demand states from pressure measurements, offering a novel approach to demand forecasting. To the best of our knowledge, this work represents a pioneering effort to address interpretability within complex WDS forecasting models. By interpreting the model, this study gained deeper insights of the internal working mechanisms of complex network prediction models. Additionally, the interpretability framework aids model refinement, improving alignment with actual WDS behavior and enhancing overall predictive and interpretative performance.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3624 text In a sustainable and cost-effective WDS, this research offers a valuable tool for efficient demand modeling for numerous cities lacking hydraulic models. Our research findings have practical implications beyond the benchmark we considered: cities seeking to optimize water distribution and enhance the management level of WDSs will benefit from the insights provided by our framework. The findings hold practical significance extending beyond the specific case studies presented: Cities aiming to optimize water distribution and improve management practices will benefit from the insights offered by our framework. Water utilities can leverage the interpretable predictions to enhance confidence in their scheduling decisions.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3625 text However, this study still has certain limitations and gaps in practical applications: (1) potential information leakage during the model tuning process when determining optimal parameters based on model performance; (2) uncertainties related to sensor acquisition errors and infrastructure conditions; and (3) the inability to satisfy all three conservation equations at the same time.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3626 container para0064\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3627 text Our open-source model lays a valuable foundation for developing machine learning-based models as an alternative to hydraulic models, which future research can further optimize. Future research should focus on enhancing the architecture of GNN and applying more interpretability methods to improve both performance and clarity. \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3628 text Model Performance:\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3629 text  First, the model should be tested on more real-world WDSs to verify its robustness and explore the relationship between network characteristics and optimal parameter settings. Additionally, more water network features should be extracted, and various types of sensor data should be integrated to achieve multi-source information integration, enhancing the model’s alignment with practical problems. Furthermore, incorporating physical laws can constrain the predictions, improving both the accuracy and credibility of the model. \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3630 text Model Interpretability:\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3631 text  The transparency of the model directly affects its deplorability in the water distribution field, which is characterized by robustness and safety. This study primarily provides model-level explanations. However, for complex model architectures, global explanations may be difficult to understand in depth. To fully comprehend the model’s outputs, future research should develop instance-level explanations that align better with human cognitive habits and are easier for non-technical users to understand. Examples of such methods include perturbation-based, gradient/feature-based, and decomposition-based approaches.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t3632 container sec0019a\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t3633 heading CRediT authorship All three neural networks were trained using the ADAM optimizer with a training batch size of 32. The initial learning rate was set to 1e-3, and it was reduced by a factor of 0.8 after 1 training epoch. If the training error does not decrease over 10 batches, the epoch will be terminated early. is to detect as many of the leaks that occurred in the 2019

a nominal model of the WDN, contained in an EPANET
hydraulic simulation input file, which has Â±10% inherent
uncertainties, due to demand and topological ambiguity.
This nominal model is used for the training of the GNN
reconstructor and predictor, as per Sec. 2.2. Competitors
are further given sensor measurements from the WDNâ€™s
control system related to the year of 2018, along with
a documented leakage dataset for 2018, of when a leak
occurred in the network, when it was fixed and its es-
timated size. The considered leakages include background
leaks measuring 1âˆ’5% of the network inflow, medium pipe
bursts representing 5 âˆ’10% of the average system inflow,
and large pipe bursts of entity > 10%. The average inflow
of the system is âˆ¼180m3/h. Both abrupt and incipient
leakages occur in the dataset. A scoring function is further
supplied in the challenge to evaluate the accuracy of the
methods, converting the scores into Euros saved from
non-revenue water and reparation costs, by the assessed
detection method. The sooner a leak is detected and the
closer it is to the actual source, the higher the economic
reward. True positives are defined as detection alarms
raised during the lifetime of a leakage and within a distance
of 300 m from the leakage location.
Equipped with this competition dataset and model, the
proposed GNN reconstructor has been trained. For what
Fig. 5. Residual signal of node 1 for the year 2018.
concerns the predictor GNN, a window of past partially
observed measurements is needed for the inference of nodal
pressures at the next time step. The size of the window T
has been selected during the training phase, considering
values of T = [1, 2, 3, 6, 12, 24], corresponding to windows
of 5 min up to 2 hr being used as the input to the GNN,
since the data is sampled at 5 min intervals. A value of
T = 3 was then selected.
The model is then tested on unseen data from the his-
torical pressure measurements of 2019. Fig. 4 shows one
inferred pressure scene for the 782 nodes, from a single
observation of 33 measurements. We then analysed node
residual signals rv(t) at each node v, which are computed
at every timestep. An example can be seen for node 1
in Fig. 5. The edge-wise residual signals of pipes in the
neighbourhood of a leak in pipe 31 is shown in Fig. 6.
As evident from Fig. 6, the signal mean values are not
equal to zero. We deduct the mean value computed at
each single pipe only from observations when the pipe
is known to be in a non-faulty state, and its neighbors
too. Edge-wise residual analysis is then performed on the
moving averages of the signals for leak detection. The
rolling means Â¯rv(t) are calculated described in the previous
section and the detection depends on the condition in
Eq. 1. There are two parameters that need to be set in the
detection method: Î± the false alarm rate parameter that
scales the detection threshold, and the size of the window
for calculating the moving average and threshold.
A window size of m = 7 days and a false alarm rate
parameter Î± = 1.0 are chosen. The leakage candidates
are filtered, so that no more than a single detection
may be returned on a given day in a neighborhood of
6 hops. The reasoning behind this approach is the in-
tuition that information of the leakage propagates the
network from the leakage origin, thus causing false-alarms
in areas non-related to the leakage. The simulation results
obtained with this configuration led to an Economic score
of e326.521, 19 True positives, 138 False positives, and
4 False negatives, as shown in Table 1. It is worth noting
that with respect to the teams in the BattleDIM challenge,
our method only uses pressure measurements, while other
entries also used flow measurements and smart meter mea-
surements. We recognise that the proposed method suffers
from false positives, with 138 false alarms given over the
year. By an initial analysis, we have observed a strong
dependency of the rate from both the topology and the

parameters, leading to propose as future works a graph-
based processing for reducing the false alarms.
4. CONCLUSIONS
In this work, a novel approach for leakage detection and
localisation in WDNs has been proposed, combining data-
driven techniques and model-based logic, where GNNs
are used to reconstruct and predict pressure values. The
discrepancy in the two modelsâ€™ outputs describes a resid-
ual signal, which is statistically analysed for changes for
leakage detection, and the embedded topological infor-
mation in the graph neural networks models is exploited
for leakage localisation. The approach is evaluated on the
BattLeDIM benchmark and obtains the highest economic
score among the contestants that competed in the chal-
lenge in 2020. The method however suffers from false
positives, resulting by the leakage effect propagating the
network. Still, the impressive economic score suggests that
the method is sensibly detecting leakages, and thus future
effort will be devoted to improve the analysis of the resid-
ual signals, to overcome this limitation. To this end, other
statistical methods for change detection, as well as neural
networks classifiers will be investigated. A strategy will
then be developed to translate the node-wise detections
into edges.

We conducted an additional investigation into the discrepancy between the masking ratios of trainâ€test pairs. Our
approach involved evaluating a trained model on the 24â€hr Oosterbeek with various masking rates rather than just
the specific rate initially trained. Through this exploration, we found that the best model of a specific testing
masking rate was unnecessary to be trained on this rate. Table 9 showed this phenomenon in extreme ratios.
Considering the tradeâ€off between sparsity and performance, the model trained with a 97% masking rate
demonstrated a Paretoâ€optimal solution. In addition, it surprisingly achieved the best results in extremely sparse
test rates (i.e., >98%). This means that at most 3% of the total nodes would be sufficient for a quality model to
monitor the Oosterbeek WDNâ€”a largeâ€scale network. Further analysis is highly recommended for WDN au-
thorities to balance the tradeâ€off between efficiency and measurement resources.
6.4. Baseline Comparison on Benchmark WDNs
In this set of experiments we compared the performance of GATResâ€small against two stateâ€ofâ€theâ€art baseline
models, GraphConvWat (HajgatÃ³ et al., 2021a, 2021b) and mGCN (Ashraf et al., 2023). We evaluated the three
models on four benchmark WDNs: Anytown, Câ€Town, Richmond, and Lâ€Town, described in Section 5.1. The
experiments were conducted following the method proposed by HajgatÃ³
et al. (2020) to ensure a fair comparison. Specifically, 1,000, 10,000, and
20,000 snapshots were generated for the Câ€Town, Lâ€Town, and Richmond
WDNs, respectively. Then, the data sets were split into training, validation,
and test sets in a 6:2:2 ratio.
We used the same experimental settings as proposed in the baseline ap-
proaches to guarantee a fair comparison. Thus, in all experiments the models
are trained for 2,000 epochs with early stopping, using the Adam gradientâ€
based optimization algorithm (Kingma & Ba, 2014). The GraphConvWat
model training was stopped if the validation loss did not improve for 50
consecutive epochs. In the case of mGCN, the training was stopped if no
improvement is seen after 250 epochs. Likewise mGCN, our model training is
stopped after 250 epochs if no improvement is observed. In all cases, it is
considered an improvement when the validation loss decreases at least
by 1eâˆ’6.

Our\\r\\napproach involved evaluating a trained model on the 24â€hr Oosterbeek with various masking rates rather than just\\r\\nthe specific rate initially trained. The\\r\\nexperiments were conducted following the method proposed by HajgatÃ³\\r\\net al. (2020) to ensure a fair comparison. The GraphConvWat\\r\\nmodel training was stopped if the validation loss did not improve for 50\\r\\nconsecutive epochs. import pandas as pd
import numpy as np


def global_preprocessing(X_data, leak_labels, time_start, time_win_len, sensors_idx=None):
    X = []
    Y = []
    y_leak = []
    
    if sensors_idx is None:
        sensors_idx = list(range(X_data.shape[1]))
    
     # Use a sliding time window to construct a labeled data set
    t_index = time_start
    time_points = range(len(leak_labels))
    i = 0
    while t_index < len(time_points) - time_win_len:
        # Grab time window from data stream
        x = X_data[t_index:t_index+time_win_len-1, sensors_idx]

        #######################
        # Feature engineering #
        #######################
        x = np.mean(x,axis=0)  # "Stupid" feature
        X.append(x)

        Y.append([X_data[t_index + time_win_len-1, n] for n in sensors_idx])

        y_leak.append(leak_labels[t_index + time_win_len-1])

        t_index += 1  # Note: Overlapping time windows
        i += 1

    X = np.array(X)
    Y = np.array(Y)
    y_leak = np.array(y_leak)

    return X, Y, y_leak
import sys
import os
import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from sklearn.metrics import mean_squared_error

from LeakDbScenarioPreprocessing import global_preprocessing
from MyModel import MyModel, create_model
from FaultDetection import EnsembleSystem
from utils import evaluate_fault_detection
from models import KNN, LinearModel
from SAM_KNN_Regression import SAMKNNRegressor


path_to_data = "data/"

time_win_len = 4  # Always consider the last four time steps
time_start = 100  # Ignore the first 100 samples

t_train_split = 3000    # Use the first 3000 sampels for training -- there are no anomalies present early until end of January!  
batch_size = 200  # Number of samples used for adapting the model to the fault


def load_data(scenario_id, use_faulty_sensor_scenario=False):
    # Open and read file
    df_pressures = pd.read_csv(os.path.join(path_to_data, f"Scenario-{scenario_id}/Results/Measurements_Pressures.csv"))
    df_flows = pd.read_csv(os.path.join(path_to_data, f"Scenario-{scenario_id}/Results/Measurements_Flows.csv"))
    
    # Parse labels
    df_labels = df_pressures[["Timestamp"]].copy()
    df_labels["label"] = 0

    def parse_leak_data(): # TODO: Assuming there is only one leaky node!
        leaky_pipe, start, end = None, None, None
        
        # Locate and load file with information on the leak
        leak_info_file = list(filter(lambda z: z.endswith(".xlsx"), os.listdir(os.path.join(path_to_data, f"Scenario-{scenario_id}/ResultsLeakages/"))))[0]
        df_leak_info = pd.read_excel(os.path.join(path_to_data, f"Scenario-{scenario_id}/ResultsLeakages/{leak_info_file}"), sheet_name="Info", engine='openpyxl')
        
        # Parse for start and end time of leak
        for _, row in df_leak_info.iterrows():
            if row["Description"] == "Leak Start":
                start = row["Value"]
            elif row["Description"] == "Leak End":
                end = row["Value"]
            elif row["Description"] == "Leak Pipe":
                leaky_pipe = row["Value"]
        
        return leaky_pipe, start, end

    leaky_pipe, start, end = parse_leak_data()

    # Load faulty sensor information
    def load_faulty_sensor_info():
        faulty_sensor_id, start, end = None, None, None

        faulty_sensor_info_file = list(filter(lambda z: z.endswith(".csv"), os.listdir(os.path.join(path_to_data, f"Scenario-{scenario_id}/ResultsSensorFaults/WithoutSensorFaults/"))))[0]
        df_faulty_sensor_info = pd.read_csv(os.path.join(path_to_data, f"Scenario-{scenario_id}/ResultsSensorFaults/WithoutSensorFaults/{faulty_sensor_info_file}"))
        for _, row in df_faulty_sensor_info.iterrows():
            if row["Description"] == "Sensor":
                faulty_sensor_id = row["Value"].replace("node", "") # Remove naming artificat from the generator
            elif row["Description"] == "Fault Start":
                start = row["Value"]
            elif row["Description"] == "Fault End":
                end = row["Value"]

        return faulty_sensor_id, start, end
    
    faulty_sensor_id = None
    if use_faulty_sensor_scenario is True:
        faulty_sensor_id, start, end = load_faulty_sensor_info()    # Note: We override start and end time of the leakage

    if use_faulty_sensor_scenario is True:  # Data from which simulation should be used?
        df_pressures = pd.read_csv(os.path.join(path_to_data, f"Scenario-{scenario_id}/ResultsSensorFaults/Measurements_Pressures.csv"))
        df_flows = pd.read_csv(os.path.join(path_to_data, f"Scenario-{scenario_id}/ResultsSensorFaults/Measurements_Flows.csv"))

    # Parse labels
    indices = df_labels[(df_labels["Timestamp"] >= start) & (df_labels["Timestamp"] <= end)].index
    for idx in indices:
        df_labels["label"].loc[idx] = 1

    labels = df_labels["label"].to_numpy().flatten()

    # Parse pressure and flow measurements
    df_pressures.drop(columns=["Timestamp"], inplace=True)
    df_flows.drop(columns=["Timestamp"], inplace=True)

    pressure_nodes = list(df_pressures.columns)
    flow_nodes = list(df_flows.columns)
    nodes = pressure_nodes + flow_nodes

    pressures_per_node = {}
    for node_id in pressure_nodes:
        pressures_per_node[node_id] = df_pressures[[node_id]].to_numpy().flatten()

    flows_per_node = {}
    for node_id in flow_nodes:
        flows_per_node[node_id] = df_flows[[node_id]].to_numpy().flatten()

    # Build numpy arrays
    y = labels
    X = np.vstack([pressures_per_node[n] for n in pressure_nodes] + [flows_per_node[n] for n in flow_nodes]).T

    return X, y, nodes, pressure_nodes, flow_nodes, leaky_pipe, faulty_sensor_id


def score(Y_True, Y_pred):
    Y_pred = Y_pred.reshape(Y_True.shape)
    return [mean_squared_error(Y_True[:, i], Y_pred[:, i]) for i in range(Y_True.shape[1])]


if __name__ == "__main__":

    if len(sys.argv) != 4:
        print("Usage: <scenario-id> <model-desc> <use_faulty_sensor_scenario>")

    # Fit model

        if model_desc == "knn":

    my_model_gen = lambda: create_model(get_model())

Without Residual Connections (woResCon). The residual connections used within each GATRes Block
are removed.
Without Mean Aggregation (woMeanAggr). The Mean Aggregation applied after the second convolution
within each GATRes Block is removed and the residual connection is added to the output of the second
convolution.
Without Residual Connection and Without Mean Aggregation (woResConâ€woAggr). Both, the residual
connection and the mean aggregation are removed from the GATRes Block.
Mean Aggregation Outside the Block (MeanAggrOut). Instead of applying a mean aggregation within
each block, it is applied only once in the forward pass, after the output of the last GATRes Block and
before the final Linear layer.
These experiments were performed by training GATResâ€small on the Câ€Town WDN. As can be seen in Table 12,
removing the Residual Connections produced the highest negative impact on model performance for all metrics.

data from the time series monitoring data, the anomalies caused by
the leakage need to be highlighted.
DMA inflow has received wide attention in leak detection given
its sensitivity for abnormal leak states. Leakage is the extra con-
sumption apart from normal user consumption of DMA, whose es-
timation is undertaken by calculating the deviation between DMA
inflow and user consumption. Therefore, the estimation of DMA
inflow, or referred to as DMA water consumption under normal
conditions, is the first procedure for leakage detection and isolation.
This section proposes a joint estimation method based on two
DMAs in the same city, one nonleakage DMA-a is used to extract
the seasonal trend of this city, and one unidentified DMA-b is used
to extract the weekly water consumption in a nonleaking state.
Based on seasonal and weekly changes, the normal water consump-
tion of DMA-b can be estimated.
For DMA-a, water consumption is a typical nonstationary time
series. Previous studies have shown that empirical mode decompo-
sition (EMD) is an efficient tool for analyzing nonlinear and non-
stationary data (Huang et al. 1998; Karthikeyan and Nagesh Kumar
2013). In this study, EMD is proposed to extract the seasonal trend
of normal water consumption. The authors assume that the water
consumption data are composed of trend items (changes within the
season), periodic items (changes within the week), and random
items (by stochastic factors), given as
YÃ°tÃž Â¼ SÃ°tÃž Ã— TÃ°tÃž Ã— RÃ°tÃž
Ã°1Ãž
where YÃ°tÃž = normal water consumption of DMA-a; SÃ°tÃž = trend
term; TÃ°tÃž = periodic term; and RÃ°tÃž = random term in the data.
It should be noted that Eq. (1) needs to be logarithmically trans-
formed before EMD. The residual term of the raw signal after EMD
represents the seasonal trend of water consumption.
For DMA-b, the nonleakage week pattern can be extracted by
the demand pattern of the hydraulic model or the average of multi-
ple nonleakage weekly demands. Based on seasonal and weekly
changes, the estimated normal water consumption in the DMA-b
can be calculated by the following method, given as
DemandDMAâˆ’b Â¼ SÃ°tÃž Ã— qÃ°tÃž
Ã°2Ãž
where qÃ°tÃž = total demand of DMA at time t. We can get the weekly
through the flow of DMA-b when there is no leakage, which also
is a water use pattern in a week. qÃ°tÃž is obtained by repeatedly
extending the pattern to one year.
Another indicator that responds to leakage is pressure. Different
from the flowmeters located at the entrance and exit of DMA, there
are generally more correlated pressure sensors in the same DMA
area (Wu and Liu 2017a; Xu et al. 2020). When a leak occurs in the
pipe network, the pressure at the node adjacent to the leak will de-
crease, and the closer the node is to the leak, the greater the pressure
drop. Similarly, when the leak is repaired, the closer the node is
to the leak, the greater the pressure rise. Therefore, the pressure
increase or decrease, that is, the pressure trend at each node, is used
as the highlight indicator of the leakage in this section.
The seasonal-trend decomposition procedure based on Loess
(STL) is proposed to estimate the pressure trend from ordinary
pressure time series (Cleveland et al. 1990). The pressure time
series P is decomposed into three series including daily compo-
nent D, trend component T, and residual component R, given as
PiÃ°tÃž Â¼ DiÃ°tÃž Ã¾ TiÃ°tÃž Ã¾ RiÃ°tÃž
Ã°3Ãž
where TiÃ°tÃž = pressure trend time series at pressure sensor i in
the DMA-b.
Identification of Leakages
Leakage is the extra consumption apart from the normal water
consumption in DMA. When a leak occurs, the SCADA system
will record online monitoring data containing the leak. The flow
and pressure caused by the leakage are highlighted in the previous
â€œEstimation of Flow and Pressureâ€ section as (1) the flow residual
between normal water consumption and the actual water consump-
tion monitored by SCADA sensors; and (2) the pressure trend at
different nodes. In this section, the authors use the presented two
features to focus on solving two identification problems: (1) the
detection of leaks; and (2) the assessment of leaks.
The purpose of leak detection is to identify the start time, end
time, and volume of leaks and to provide zonal (or DMA-level) leak
tips for the water utility. Previous studies have suggested that leak-
age is more sensitive to flow than to pressure (Mounce et al. 2011;
Ye and Fenner 2011). Considering the timeliness and accuracy of
leak identification, the flow residual series between normal water
consumption and the actual water consumption monitored by
SCADA sensors is used for solving the first leak detection problem.
For flow residual data, the characteristics of the flow residual
sequence should be analyzed first. Although the residuals series is
calculated by the difference between observed and estimated series,
it is still a time series with irregular fluctuations. To reduce the non-
stationary residual series and amplify the leakage signal implicit
in the residual series, a multiscale difference method is proposed
in this study. Difference operation, also known as finite difference,
difference function, or difference operation, is a concept in math-
ematics. It maps the original function fÃ°xÃž to fÃ°x Ã¾ aÃž âˆ’fÃ°x Ã¾ bÃž.
Difference operation, corresponding to differential operation, is an
important concept in calculus, which is commonly used for the
smoothing of nonstationary time series (Flajolet and Sedgewick
1995). In this study, in order to eliminate the change of the mean
value and highlight the mutation of the sequence over time, the
difference value Î”rÃ°xkÃž of the continuous items in the residual
sequence is calculated by Eqs. (4)â€“(7)
Î”r1Ã°xkÃž Â¼ rÃ°xkÃž âˆ’rÃ°xkâˆ’1Ãž
Ã°4Ãž
Î”rhourÃ°xkÃž Â¼ rÃ°xkÃž âˆ’rÃ°xkâˆ’1 hourÃž
Ã°5Ãž
Î”rdayÃ°xkÃž Â¼ rÃ°xkÃž âˆ’rÃ°xkâˆ’1 dayÃž
Ã°6Ãž
Î”rweekÃ°xkÃž Â¼ rÃ°xkÃž âˆ’Ã°xkâˆ’1 weekÃž
Ã°7Ãž
The relationship between flow residuals and leakage should be
analyzed. In WDSs, the leakages are usually divided into two types:
abrupt leakage and incipient leakage. Corresponding to residual
data, the pattern of residual series contains constant (no leak),
abrupt increase (abrupt leakage), incipient increase (incipient
leakage), and abrupt decrease (repaired leakage, the end time of
leakage). To identify the leakage signal in the residual difference
sequence, statistical process control (SPC) methods are proposed in
this study. The abrupt increase and decrease are easily identified
through difference transformation, and the three-sigma SPC limit
is used to identify the sudden rise and incline of the flow curve
(Jung et al. 2015; Wang et al. 2020). The three-sigma SPC method
is analyzed by Z score, which is a statistical indicator for outlier
detection. Z score indicates how many standard deviations a data
point is from the average of the sample, given as

prises a pump and water tank to supply enough pressure to the
consumers in that area and is also equipped with 82 water demand
reading sensors. Also, 33 pressure sensors and 2 flow sensors
were deployed across the most sensitive node locations within the
network. These historical data of L-town have been collected for
the period January 1, 2018 0:00 until December 31, 2019 23:55
at 5-min time steps. Several leakages occurred in L-town since
the 2018 datasets have a clear leak label, data in 2018 were used
for model development, and data in 2019 were used for testing
the EILF.
Performance of Estimation
According to the layout of the L-town water distribution network
and the locations of the sensors, the network is divided into two
relatively independent areas. Area C has sensors for total inflow,
and most nodes in the area have water meters (AMRs) at the same
time, which is regarded as one zone. Area A and B are treated as
Fig. 5. Spatial distribution of L-town network.
Fig. 4. Comparison of leak detection between raw residual series and 5-min difference residual series: (a) the Z score of raw residual series and 5-min
difference residual series; (b) the statistical boxes of raw residual series and 5-min difference residual series; and (c) the statistical boxes of Z score
calculated by raw residual series and 5-min difference residual series.

=== PDF PAGE 7 ===
one zone, which is called area AB, because there is no separate
sensor measuring the inflow to area B. The flow residuals in
area C and area AB were calculated separately.
For area C, the difference between the total inflow and total
water consumption can reflect the leak information. The total in-
flow is calculated via the water level in the tank and the flow data of
the pump that delivers water to the Tank. It should be noted that the
level data of the tank has not changed before and after some time
point, and the pump at this time also stopped running. This seems to
suggest no water usage in the area, yet it contradicts the data from
the AMRs that show evident water consumption in that period. This
implies inaccuracy in the level data of the tower. To minimize its
adverse impact on the detection of leakage events in area C, the
total inflow and total demand in area C are calculated at intervals
of 30 min.
The total inflow to the area AB is recorded by three flow sen-
sors. The actual water consumption of users in the area AB needs
to be estimated. Considering the relatively complete monitoring of
junction consumption behavior in area C, this can be used as the
nonleakage DMA data to extract the seasonal trend of the L-town.
Specifically, the changing trend of usersâ€™ water usage mode in
area C during the year should be similar to that in area AB.
Therefore, the seasonal trend in the data in area C can be trans-
planted to the prediction of water demand in area AB. The weekly
pattern of area AB can be predicted by combining it with the sim-
ulation in one week of the hydraulic model. The main solution here
is to analyze the trend of demand mode in area C, the flow series of
2018 and 2019 are decomposed by EMD. The seasonal change
trend of water consumption obtained by EMD is shown in Fig. 6.
The figure also shows the value of the 1st/3rd/5th intrinsic mode
function (IMF) (Hu et al. 2021). Each IMF represents the compo-
nent of a certain frequency characteristic in the original signal.
The interannual variation of water consumption can be expressed
by residual. From the interannual point of view, the growth of
water consumption is slow, and the water consumption in winter
is less than that in summer. After that, the inflow could be estimated
through the EMD seasonal component and demand pattern of
DMA, and the result of estimation is shown in Fig. 7. Besides,
the time series trend decomposition model to estimate the trend
item of the pressure series. The pressure trend series is decomposed
by additive mode STL. When different leaks occur, the pressures
of multiple nodes will change in different magnitudes, as shown
in Fig. 8.
Performance of Identification
The pressure and flow changes resulting from the leak are described
by the estimated flow residual series and pressure trend series.
For the flow of area AB, it is intuitive to calculate the residuals
between inflow and estimated flow for identifying leakages. For
the flow of area C, data observed from the pump sensor and the
tank level meter are used to calculate the total inflow of area C,
and the sum of AMR demands gives the aggregate user consump-
tions. The difference between total inflow and user consumptions is
used to identify leakages as residuals in area C.

After that, the inflow could be estimated\\r\\nthrough the EMD seasonal component and demand pattern of\\r\\nDMA, and the result of estimation is shown in Fig. 7. Besides,\\r\\nthe time series trend decomposition model to estimate the trend\\r\\nitem of the pressure series. The pressure trend series is decomposed\\r\\nby additive mode STL. When different leaks occur, the pressures\\r\\nof multiple nodes will change in different magnitudes, as shown\\r\\nin Fig. 8.\\r\\nPerformance of Identification\\r\\nThe pressure and flow changes resulting from the leak are described\\r\\nby the estimated flow residual series and pressure trend series.\\r\\nFor the flow of area AB, it is intuitive to calculate the residuals\\r\\nbetween inflow and estimated flow for identifying leakages. For\\r\\nthe flow of area C, data observed from the pump sensor and the\\r\\ntank level meter are used to calculate the total inflow of area C,\\r\\nand the sum of AMR demands gives the aggregate user consump-\\r\\ntions. The difference between total inflow and user consumptions is\\r\\nused to identify leakages as residuals in area C.\\r\\n\"}"

def leak_analysis(nodes,scada_data,cor_time_frame):
    N = len(nodes)
    T = scada_data.pressures.shape[0]
    P = scada_data.pressures[nodes].values
    V = scada_data.flows['PUMP_1'].values
    
    K0 = np.zeros((N,N))
    K1 = np.zeros((N,N))
    Kd = np.zeros((N,N))
    
    ref_nodes = []
    for i,node in enumerate(nodes):
        ref_node = Ref_node(node)
        models=[]

        X_tr = np.concatenate([scada_data.pressures[node].loc[cor_time_frame[0]:cor_time_frame[1]].to_numpy().reshape(-1,1),
                               scada_data.flows['PUMP_1'].loc[cor_time_frame[0]:cor_time_frame[1]].to_numpy().reshape(-1,1)],
                             axis=1)

        for j,node_cor in enumerate(nodes):
            y_tr = scada_data.pressures[node_cor].loc[cor_time_frame[0]:cor_time_frame[1]].to_numpy().reshape(-1,1)
            model = LinearRegression()
            models.append(model.fit(X_tr, y_tr))
            K0[i,j] = model.intercept_[0]
            K1[i,j] = model.coef_[0][0]
            Kd[i,j] = model.coef_[0][1]
        ref_node.set_models(models)
    
    np.fill_diagonal(K0,0)
    np.fill_diagonal(K1,1)
    np.fill_diagonal(Kd,0)
    
    e = np.multiply.outer(K0,np.ones(T))\
        + np.multiply.outer(Kd,V)\
        + np.multiply.outer(K1,np.ones(T))*np.multiply.outer(P,np.ones(N)).transpose(1,2,0)\
        - np.multiply.outer(P,np.ones(N)).transpose(2,1,0)
    
    if 'n215' in nodes:
        e[nodes.index('n215'),:,:] *= 0.02
    
    res = np.zeros((N,T))
    for t in range(T):
        e_t_i = e[:,:,t].copy()
        e_t_i[e_t_i<0]=0
        e_t_i[e_t_i>0]=1
        i_ = pd.Series(e_t_i.sum(axis=0)).sort_values(ascending=False).index.to_list()
        i_max = int(i_[0])
        
        res[i_max,t] = np.linalg.norm(e[:,:,t][:,i_max])
        
    MRE = pd.DataFrame(res.T,index=scada_data.pressures.index,columns=nodes)
    return MRE

    def forward(self, x, edge_index, batch=None, edge_attr=None):
        for i in range(self.num_blocks):
            x = self.blocks[i](x, edge_index) 
        return x


class GIN(torch.nn.Module):
    def __init__(self,name='GIN_bottleneck',num_blocks = 10, nc = 32, in_channels=1, out_channels=1):
        super(GIN, self).__init__()
        self.name = f'{name}_{num_blocks}b_{nc}c'
        
        self.num_blocks = num_blocks

        blocks= []

        for i in range(self.num_blocks):
            if i == 0 :
                layer = GINConv(MLP(dims=[in_channels,nc//2,nc]),eps=0.0)
            elif i == self.num_blocks - 1:
                layer = GINConv(Linear(nc,out_channels,bias=False),eps=0.0)
            else:
                layer = GINConv(MLP(dims=[nc,nc//2,nc]),eps=0.0)
            blocks.append(layer)
        self.blocks = torch.nn.ModuleList(blocks)
       

    
    def forward(self, x, edge_index, batch=None, edge_attr=None):
        for i in range(self.num_blocks):
            o=x
            x = self.blocks[i](x, edge_index)
            if x.shape[-1] == o.shape[-1]:
              x = x + o
        return x

#REF: https://github.com/HammerLabML/GCNs_for_WDS
class MLP(Sequential):
    def __init__(self, dims: List[int], bias: bool = True, dropout: float = 0., activ=SELU()):
        m = []
        for i in range(1, len(dims)):
            m.append(Linear(dims[i - 1], dims[i], bias=bias))

            if i < len(dims) - 1:                
                m.append(activ)
                m.append(Dropout(dropout))

        super().__init__(*m)

class GENConvolution(MessagePassing):
    r"""
    Args:
        in_dim (int or tuple): Size of each input sample, or :obj:`-1` to
            derive the size from the first input(s) to the forward method.
            A tuple corresponds to the sizes of source and target
            dimensionalities.
        out_dim (int): Size of each output sample.
        edge_dim (int): Size of edge features.
        aggr (str, optional): The aggregation scheme to use (:obj:`"softmax"`,
            :obj:`"softmax_sg"`, :obj:`"power"`, :obj:`"add"`, :obj:`"mean"`,
            :obj:`max`). (default: :obj:`"softmax"`)        
        num_layers (int, optional): The number of MLP layers.
            (default: :obj:`2`)
        eps (float, optional): The epsilon value of the message construction
            function. (default: :obj:`1e-7`)
        bias (bool, optional): If set to :obj:`False`, will not use bias. 
            (default: :obj:`True`)
        dropout (float, optional): Percentage of neurons to be dropped in MLP.
            (default: :obj:`0.`)
        **kwargs (optional): Additional arguments of
            :class:`torch_geometric.nn.conv.GenMessagePassing`.
    Shapes:
        - **input:**
          node features :math:`(|\mathcal{V}|, F_{in})` or
          :math:`((|\mathcal{V_s}|, F_{in}), (|\mathcal{V_t}|, F_{t})`
          if bipartite,
          edge indices :math:`(2, |\mathcal{E}|)`,
          edge attributes :math:`(|\mathcal{E}|, D)` *(optional)*
        - **output:** node features :math:`(|\mathcal{V}|, F_{out})` or
          :math:`(|\mathcal{V}_t|, F_{out})` if bipartite
    """
    def __init__(self, in_dim: int, out_dim: int, edge_dim: int,
                 aggr: str = 'add', num_layers: int = 2, eps: float = 1e-7, 
                 bias: bool = True, dropout: float = 0., **kwargs):

        kwargs.setdefault('aggr', None)
        super().__init__(**kwargs)

        self.in_dim = in_dim
        self.out_dim = out_dim
